# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-29

| **[GEPA-Curator: Curated Learning for Self Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37651v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in multi-turn agentic workloads that require sustained interaction with complex environments. Existing reflective prompt optimizers such as GEPA learn from failed rollouts, but agentic failures can also come from prompt weaknesses, harness limitations, stochastic execution, or noisy judges. We argue that such failures may not provide useful learning signal, and may even be harmful; and thus it is essential to curate which tasks are provided to the prompt optimizer for better performance. We introduce GEPA-Curator, a framework that augments the prompt optimizer to select which agent experiences should shape prompt optimization. In particular, GEPA-Curator consists of two components: task curation, which filters out tasks that are likely to provide noisy or unreliable learning signals, and prior success retrieval, which selectively retrieves past successful trajectories for a task and provides them as additional context to the prompt optimizer. We evaluate GEPA-Curator on GoBrowse, ALFWorld, AppWorld, and $τ$-bench, using both Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 5 as optimizer models. GEPA-Curator matches or exceeds the mean performance of both baselines, with gains of up to 20.7% over GEPA and 21.2% over ACE.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scaling Laws for Tool-Use Agents via Controllable Environment Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37648v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We explore how to build performant large language model (LLM) tool-use agents that complete complex, multi-step tasks. Specifically, we study how data composition in synthetically generated tool-use environments affects the training of such agents. To do so, we propose the SynthAPI pipeline, which explicitly controls the diversity of generated environments along the axes of tools, tasks, and the databases on which the tools operate. By systematically varying SynthAPI's generation controls in 458,752 tasks across 849 trained models of four different sizes, we derive a scaling law that predicts downstream tool-use agent generalization based on the scale and composition of the synthesized environments. These predictable relationships reveal several lessons for scaling tool-use agent data. As data budgets grow, scaling tool and database diversity becomes more effective than increasing the number of tasks, and increasing data complexity yields larger gains. Scaling according to these findings yields agents that outperform models trained on data from prior work in large-scale tool-use agent and synthetic environment generation across four evaluation benchmarks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Metacognitive Decoding: Efficient Inference for Diffusion Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37642v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models offer a promising path to efficient agents through parallel token generation. However, efficient interaction requires adapting both where computation is spent and how much text is generated. We introduce a metacognitive controller that guides diffusion generation according to the needs of the current response and the agent's interaction with the environment. It learns when to finalize tokens and how far generation should extend, reducing overall computation within responses. Our controller, a lightweight plug-in for the diffusion backbone, is trained on agent trajectories to balance correctness and generation cost. This approach connects the agent's generation process to the interaction the agent serves. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that our approach reduces inference FLOPs by 20.6% on average compared with the most efficient baseline, while maintaining competitive task accuracy. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of our approach.</p></details> | 10 pages, 4 figures |
| **[Annotrace: A Workbench for Step-Level Annotation of Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23284v3)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every study of why LLM agents fail begins by labelling traces by hand, and every such study builds its own throwaway tool. Observability platforms record agent traces but offer no structured labelling; annotation tools label text but have no notion of a step, a delegation or a tool call, leaving it unclear what the smallest unit of execution to label should be. Annotrace reads six trace formats, including OpenTelemetry GenAI, LangSmith and Langfuse, and turns each run into an ordered, nested list of steps. The annotator writes their own labelling codebook; a frontier model proposes a starting set from a sample of their own traces, answers first, and is asked exactly the questions the human is asked, making annotation a joint human-AI process. Annotrace measures agreement between any two annotators, human or AI. On a multi-agent question-answering corpus it reproduces a previous manual effort's 235 annotation units exactly, and two models labelling the same traces agree 91% of the time at $κ= 0.82$.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 4 figures, 1 table. Under review at the AAAI 2027 Demonstration Program</p></details> |
| **[Agentic Distributional Regression: Conditional Density Estimation through Agent-Written Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37611v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper we propose a method for estimating conditional densities from an iid sample using a language-model agent. The agent writes the estimator as a short program that, for each covariate value, returns the estimated conditional distribution of the response. The program is built from a library of probability distributions, so the returned law can be a parametric distribution, a mixture or a multivariate factorization, with parameters given by explicit functions of the covariates. Since the number of operations and constants in the program is limited, the fitted conditional density is a formula that can be inspected and evaluated independently of the agent, while the choice of the model structure and of the fitting procedure is left to the agent. We call this approach agentic distributional regression (ADR). Treating the returned program as a random conditional density estimator, we derive a finite-sample error bound under explicit complexity and optimization assumptions, and an oracle inequality for selection using independent validation data. For a stably selected regular structure with sufficiently accurate fitting, we obtain asymptotic normality of the fitted constants. In simulation studies, on twelve real datasets and on a new dataset collected after the agents' training data, ADR with Claude Opus 5.5 has the best average performance among the methods considered.</p></details> |  |
| **[EnvSched: Environment-Aware Scheduling for Rollout-Efficient Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37609v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As executable environments for language agents scale, generating and verifying their rollouts becomes increasingly expensive. Under finite rollout budgets, this raises an important question: can data management at the environment level make agentic reinforcement learning (RL) more efficient by organizing, evaluating, and scheduling task instances together with the environments that determine their tools, states, transitions, and verification? To address this question, we introduce EnvSched (Environment-aware Scheduling), an online scheduler that learns from rollout history. EnvSched assigns scheduling value through two complementary modeling choices: exploitation prioritizes tasks likely to provide informative reward differences, while coverage spreads each batch across underobserved environments and interaction patterns. EnvSched organizes environment--task pairs as nodes in a dual-relation graph, with within-environment edges capturing task semantics and shared tool, state, and verifier structure, and cross-environment edges capturing abstract execution and reward topology. By propagating historical rollout statistics along both edge types, it predicts within-task reward variance. These predictions guide a scheduler that allocates each batch to tasks with high predicted reward variance and underobserved environments and tasks. Environment quotas and a similarity penalty further limit concentration and redundancy, yielding informative and diverse batches without additional pilot rollouts. In RL on Qwen3-8B, EnvSched achieves an average score of 48.81 across BFCL-v3, tau-Bench, and ACEBench-Agent after 40 training steps, exceeding the highest score attained by random task selection after 120 steps. It also improves downstream performance across model sizes at the same training budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[Detailed balance in large language model-driven agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10047v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-driven agents are emerging as a powerful new paradigm for solving complex problems. Despite the empirical success of these practices, a theoretical framework to understand and unify their macroscopic dynamics remains lacking. This paper proposes a method based on the least action principle to estimate the underlying generative directionality of LLMs embedded within agents. By experimentally measuring the transition probabilities between LLM-generated states, we report evidence consistent with a tendency toward detailed balance in LLM-generated transitions, indicating that LLM generation may not be achieved by generally learning rule sets and strategies, but rather by implicitly learning a class of underlying potential functions that may transcend different LLM architectures and prompt templates. To our knowledge, this provides an effective macroscopic description of LLM generative dynamics that does not depend on specific model details. This work is an attempt to establish a macroscopic dynamics theory of complex AI systems, aiming to elevate the study of AI agents from a collection of engineering practices to a science built on effective measurements that are predictable and quantifiable.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 19 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[TANDEM: Balancing Centralized and Decentralized Critics to Address the Information Gap in Complex Multi-Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37592v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning under centralized training with decentralized execution (CTDE) shapes decentralized policies with a centralized critic that observes the global state, then removes the critic at execution. The policies are optimized to rely on information they will not have when they act, an information gap between training and execution. It widens under partial observability, observation noise, and openness, where the set of agents and tasks present changes during an episode. Existing methods transfer centralized guidance in one direction only, removing or distilling the critic once, so the policies never rehearse acting without the critic while they can still be corrected. We propose TANDEM, which keeps one centralized critic and reads it two ways at each training step: a centralized read from the global state, and a decentralized read restricted to the information each agent will have at execution. A parameter-free gate compares the two reads for each agent and trains the policy on the decentralized read where they agree, reinstating the centralized read only where the local view is misleading. A shared target pulls the decentralized read toward the centralized one. We formalize the setting as a task- and agent-open decentralized partially observable Markov decision process (TAO-Dec-POMDP) and evaluate TANDEM on three cooperative domains, each isolating one form of the information gap: discovery for partial observability, sampling for observation noise, and openness in wildfire suppression. We compare against six CTDE baselines and ablate the gate and the shared target; the supplementary material holds the full variant sweep, diagnostics, and learning curves. We measure return at execution, once the centralized critic is removed. TANDEM is the best method on eight of the nine setups and never significantly worse than any baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[TeamGym: A Gym for Team Decision-Making by AI Agents with Distributed Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37571v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many real-world decisions require teams to integrate information distributed across participants. Yet AI agents are still primarily trained and benchmarked in isolation. We introduce TeamGym, an environment for evaluating and training language model-based agents on team decision-making problems constructed from real-world legal, finance, medical, and software engineering corpora. TeamGym introduces a method to identify a minimal set of facts, each element of which is necessary and all of which are collectively sufficient for a correct final decision, which we call pivotal information. This enables TeamGym to both control the distribution of pivotal information and track how well agents identify and integrate pivotal information when making team decisions. Examining 8 language models on 12 source corpora, we find that current AI agents often fail to effectively share information: Distributing pivotal information across participants lowers success rate by 22.9 percentage points on average relative to giving the same information to a single model (a communication gap). We also document that participants do not effectively retrieve decision-relevant information on their own: A team's success rate is lowered by a further 19.9 points when participants must identify and extract pivotal information from source material rather than receiving it directly (a retrieval gap). To help remedy these deficits, we use TeamGym to train policies through reinforcement learning on team decision loss. Across two policy-training experiments, we find that training improves final-decision quality and induces emergent coordination in participation, speaker selection, and communication, even though the reward evaluates only the final decision.</p></details> |  |
| **[Gym-Anything-RT: Scaling Real-Time Environments for Vision-Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37567v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents have made substantial progress in domains where they have a non-trivial amount of time to deliberate between actions, such as mathematics, software engineering, and performing tasks on desktop applications. It is less clear how such models fare in settings that demand real-time interaction, such as playing a video game or operating a physical device. In these settings, the environment state may evolve as the agent is reasoning, or even as the underlying model is performing a forward pass. To gain insight into the real-time interaction capabilities of vision-language agents, we introduce Gym-Anything-RT. Our key idea is to construct visual minigames that demand a portfolio of capabilities required for interactive real-time settings. Gym-Anything-RT uses an agentic pipeline to automatically generate such environments based on a taxonomy of six capabilities required for real-time interaction, including the ability to perceive the environment and act under an evolving environment state. A key feature of Gym-Anything-RT is that it allows for controlled comparison of real-time interaction capabilities: we can run the same minigame in real time, or pause or slow down the environment to relax the requirement for real-time actions. We use Gym-Anything-RT to construct 100 real-time minigames and evaluate AI agents on them. We find large capability gaps in real-time interaction. For example, GPT-6 Astra (xHigh) achieves a pass rate of only 9.0% on the real-time minigames, which rises to 75.0% when the environment is paused. We also identify intriguing behaviors by the most powerful frontier agents: for instance, when given the ability to write code, these agents write code that bypasses the need to perceive and act in real time. We publicly release Gym-Anything-RT and the generated minigames to spur further research on AI agents in real-time interactive settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[EvoIn: Bridging Evolution and Internalization for Agent Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35290v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent work has explored improving agents by jointly evolving their harnesses and models, but often takes a ''potpourri'' approach that bundles together new tools, new decision-making procedures, and model adaptation to the evolved harness under a single notion of agent improvement. In this paper, we instead investigate how agents can improve their decision-making procedures. In particular, we propose EvoIn, an agent fine-tuning framework that bridges evolution and internalization. EvoIn first analyzes agent execution traces to evolve and validate new decision-making procedures by temporarily instantiating them in the harness. The validated procedures guide the agent to generate improved reasoning traces. These traces are then rewritten into self-contained reasoning traces, removing explicit references to harness instructions while expressing the induced decision logic as the model's own reasoning. Finally, EvoIn fine-tunes the model on the rewritten traces, internalizing these procedures so that the improved decision-making persists without the evolved harness at inference time. We evaluate EvoIn on diverse benchmarks and find that it consistently enables agents to learn stronger decision-making procedures, raising the pass rate by 10.9 points in-domain and by 9.2 points out-of-domain. Results further show that the internalized decision procedures generalize to unseen tasks. Case studies show that agents can learn to decide how to solve a task before solving it, for example by checking a document's length to choose between reading it in full and searching it. EvoIn is also broadly applicable, showing consistent improvements on another model family.</p></details> | 36 pages, 3 figures |
| **[VibeVideoBench: Evaluating Multimodal Agents on Code-Driven Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35864v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion video models produce striking clips, but they cannot reliably render an exact formula, a correct chart, a rule-consistent game or narration synchronized to on-screen events. A different route is emerging: multimodal agents that write code to render every frame and synthesize every sound. We introduce VibeVideoBench, a benchmark for evaluating agents on code-driven video generation. Its 239 self-contained tasks in English and Chinese span 12 categories, from data stories and music-driven pieces to classroom lessons and paper explainers; each asks an agent, running in its native harness inside a sandbox with no image or video generation model, to deliver a finished MP4 with sound within one hour. Evaluation is hierarchical and evidence-based: program checks of the delivery spec, about 13k task-specific rubric checks traced to the requirements they enforce, and five quality dimensions anchored per task, scored by three VLM judge panels. Across nine frontier agent systems and 2,151 scored rollouts, GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5.5 rank first and second with both the judges and human annotators, and the strongest system satisfies every core requirement on 76.3% of tasks. Delivery is a second axis: counting missing videos as zero changes the ranking, and three quarters of the lost videos are final renders that started too late, ran too slowly or never started, a failure of how agents plan their hour. Agents build their own rendering pipelines and inspect their own frames, yet how much they look does not predict quality. The judges' ranking of the systems closely matches blind human preferences.</p></details> |  |
| **[Pretraining Knows What, Simulation Teaches How: Compositional Generalization in a Sim-Trained Robot Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35817v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot policy needs to understand what a task is asking for in a scene, and how to move the robot to accomplish that task. To achieve these two skills, we introduce Zero-real Agent Policy (ZAP), a recipe that teaches a small off-the-shelf vision-language model to control a robot via a simple tool harness. We use a pretrained 27B parameter model to bootstrap world understanding and reasoning, followed by supervised fine-tuning and GRPO on simple simulated tasks to teach it how to move the robot. Without observing real-world robot data during training, ZAP reaches 96.7% success on real-world tasks, matching GPT-6 Astra in our harness and outperforming the MolmoAct2 VLA (33.3%), while running 3x faster and 17x cheaper than GPT-6 Astra. Furthermore, ZAP also generalizes to an unseen embodiment as well as new objects, goals and strategies never seen during training. On a single-arm Franka, ZAP reaches 55.9% on RoboLab-120, 4.9 percentage points ahead of the leading VLA/WAM baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Interaction to Capability: Task Advancement as State and Supervision for GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37482v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>GUI memory should preserve the results of past actions and learn reusable operations. This is difficult in long-horizon workflows, where agents must use information across applications and useful operations are interleaved with failed attempts. We introduce Task Advancement Memory (TAM), which defines task advancement as verified task progress, and supports ongoing task execution and learns reusable operations. During execution, Task Advancement State represents how verified task progress changes the remaining goals, allowing the agent to build on earlier achievements across applications. After each task, advancement supervision identifies and abstracts the interactions that produced a verified local result. The learned operations preserve the relation between required inputs, expected outcomes, and verification conditions. This retains locally successful operations from both successful and failed tasks. On MobileWorld GUI-only tasks, Advancement State alone raises success from 12.0% to 28.2%. Full TAM achieves a 17.6% relative gain in task success over the strongest evaluated baseline and reduces lifecycle tokens per solved task by 38.7%. On SPA-Bench, TAM achieves state-of-the-art task success in the single-app English Level 3 setting.</p></details> |  |
| **[Concordat: Typed Sessions for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37461v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents built on large language models are increasingly allowed to take consequential actions: cancelling bookings, issuing refunds, changing accounts. The rules that govern those actions, such as "obtain the customer's explicit confirmation before changing anything", are usually stated in the prompt and followed only as reliably as the model chooses to follow them. We present Concordat, a Python library that treats an agent as an object whose interface is a finite-state protocol, in the spirit of typestate and session types, and enforces that protocol on the model at run time. On every turn the model is offered only the tools that are legal in the current protocol state, calls outside that set are rejected before they have side effects, decisions reserved for humans can only be requested, and token and tool-call budgets are charged and split linearly across handoffs. Protocols are checked when they are defined, and a subclass of an agent is rejected if it could bypass its parent's protocol, a behavioural form of Liskov substitution. We evaluate a Concordat agent against the standard agent of tau-bench on its 50 airline tasks, adding simulated customers who pressure the agent to skip confirmation. The standard agent made changes without the required "yes" in 13 of 50 tasks with gpt-4.1 and in 29 of 128 task-runs with Claude Haiku 5.5; the Concordat agent made none, with no significant difference in task success. tau-bench's own reward did not detect most of these violations. Concordat is open source.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 1 figure, 1 table. Code, benchmark harness and results: https://github.com/miladsikaroudi/concordat</p></details> |
| **[M4Bench: Evaluating Procedural Specification for Clinical EHR Derivation Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37459v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical research over electronic health records depends on derived variables whose definitions sit outside the schema, in item codes, time windows, missingness rules, and output-grain choices that change cohorts and downstream conclusions. We introduce M4Bench, 28 clinical derivation tasks across 15 task families on MIMIC-IV and eICU, to test whether AI coding agents can reproduce such derivations and how clinician-reviewed agent skills change their results. Its controls decompose the value of skills into specification completeness, delivery format, and schema familiarity. In a Docker-isolated Codex campaign (GPT 5.4 Mini and GPT 5.5), skills raise reward by 0.136 over the no-skill baseline, with 26 of 28 tasks improving. Specification completeness accounts for most of this gain: on an eight-task control subset, an operational spec alone raises reward by 0.162, and skills add only 0.024 on top, indistinguishable from zero. On a ten-task subset, decoy skills score like no skill and raw reference SQL nearly matches targeted skills, arguing against generic context or the skill format as the explanation. Schema obfuscation and restructuring preserve 86% and 70% of the skill gain (GPT 5.4 Mini, 26 MIMIC-IV tasks), a descriptive robustness check rather than a bound on memorization. A descriptive Claude Code replication on all 28 tasks (Opus 4.7 and Sonnet 4.6, 560 runs) is directionally consistent (+0.120, 21 of 28 tasks improving). These gains are partial agreement with the reference, not completed tasks: only 25 of 280 with-skill and 13 of 280 no-skill Codex runs pass every strict check, and skills barely change which rows agents return (key F1 +0.003). Skills are released as a versioned, clinician-reviewable markdown delivery format, with a family-level reference-validity audit. M4Bench releases tasks, skills, evaluation code, and sanitized per-run evidence as an open-source benchmark.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track). 10 pages main text, 33 pages total. Code: https://github.com/hannesill/m4/tree/m4bench-neurips2026-camera-ready/benchmark . Evidence: https://huggingface.co/datasets/hannesill/m4bench-evidence</p></details> |
| **[Test-Time Communication in Trading Agent Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37448v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Communication between agents has mostly been studied on tasks with a shared objective and fast, clear feedback. Trading has neither: each agent manages its own account, with decisions being scored by markets late and noisily. A trading swarm is thus a population of independent decision-makers rather than a team, for which convergence is a risk and not the goal. We ran 200 agents from four model families in groups of ten under five communication regimes, from isolation to message boards and shared notes, over seven US trading days (1 to 9 October 2026), logging everything each agent read before every decision. In two of the four families, communicating groups came to hold far more of the same stocks than the isolated group. A group's return was largely the return on what its members held in common. Seeing a post from a groupmate made an agent 7 percentage points more likely to reuse an eight-word phrase from it. Agents that started too close together to see each other's posts shared such a phrase in 14% of cases, the rate that common inputs produce. Seeing the post did not detectably change that morning's trades. Case studies traced the adoption of positions and decision rules over subsequent days, and communicating groups sold in step more often than isolated ones. Because isolated groups also converged on the same stocks, sometimes completely, assessments of herding need to measure excess convergence relative to a no-peer baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 10 figures, 10 tables</p></details> |
| **[Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning of Co-Adaptive Hip Exoskeleton Control (MARL-Exo) for Metabolic Energy Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37441v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Objective: This study aims to determine whether a hip-exoskeleton policy trained in simulation, by co-adapting with a musculoskeletal human model under a metabolic-energy reward, reduces the metabolic cost of walking in new users without per-user tuning, and how mechanical-power shaping affects the learned assistance. Methods: We developed MARL-Exo, a multi-agent reinforcement learning framework in which a full-body human model (304 muscle actuators; randomized anatomy, gait, and speed) and a bilateral hip-exoskeleton policy are trained concurrently in two stages. The exoskeleton observes only delayed hip kinematics and its past commands; its reward includes muscle metabolic power from a physiologically based energetics model, alone (Metabolic) or with mechanical-power shaping (Metabolic + Power). The Metabolic policy was deployed and tested without retraining in eight healthy adults walking at 1.25 m/s, using indirect calorimetry. Results: In simulation, the Metabolic policy reduced modeled muscle metabolic power by 13.8% relative to no-device walking, versus 8.8% for Metabolic + Power. On hardware, MARL-Exo reduced net metabolic rate by 18.3 $\pm$ 6.6% relative to Passive in all eight participants. Relative to No-Exo, the mean reduction was 7.4 $\pm$ 10.0%; six participants more than offset the 13.1% device-wearing penalty, walking up to 24.2% below their No-Exo cost. Conclusion: A metabolically informed policy co-adapted in simulation transferred to users without per-user tuning and consistently reduced the metabolic cost of walking with the device, while explicit power shaping reduced the simulated benefit. Significance: Unlike per-user human-in-the-loop optimization, this approach yields a single population-level controller and allows assistance objectives to be screened in simulation before human testing.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 6 figures, including supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[FeatureArc: Benchmarking Coding Agents on Evolving Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37434v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A software feature usually evolves over time: developers extend its first version as new use cases and requirements arrive, and each extension builds on code written earlier. Most feature benchmarks evaluate a single request on a fixed codebase. Feature evolution requires evaluating successive extensions on an agent's own earlier code and checking whether the feature remains correct. We introduce FeatureArc to evaluate coding agents on evolving features, together with a pipeline that constructs its tasks from repository histories. The benchmark combines dependencies between successive stages of the same feature, explicit requirements and interface contracts, and executable checks across four programming languages. The pipeline validates fail-to-pass (F2P) tests for new behavior and pass-to-pass (P2P) tests for existing behavior on the reference code before and after each stage. We evaluated 10 coding agents on 73 manually reviewed tasks of 2 to 10 stages. Each agent works in one persistent session and workspace without external test feedback or rollback. The evaluation reveals a gap between first-stage and whole-feature success: pooled success falls from 62.7% at the first stage to 28.5% when the whole feature is tested on the final code. Trajectory analysis identifies inherited defects as a common failure mechanism, present in 72.9% of failed later stages. These earlier errors often remain undetected: in stages following a failure, agents leave the earlier error unnoticed about 95% of the time, and their self-tests rarely revisit earlier stages. Evolving a feature autonomously, without human checks between stages, therefore remains a substantial challenge for current coding agents. At Tencent, the pipeline extends internal evaluations to this setting and guides the development of Hunyuan, Tencent's family of large language models.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 8 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[The Envelope Layer: An Underestimated Surface of LLM Agent Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37415v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prior work framed the envelope layer as the channel through which malicious content enters an agent, and established it as a defense surface independent of the LLM layer and the agent layer; however, that work only tested the envelope layer briefly and did not examine how label strength, envelope authenticity, scaffold differences, or existing memories affect defense effectiveness. Following its GDA Measurement methodology and the A/B/C/D outcome taxonomy, this paper takes the A2A-TIBA attack as the test vehicle, uses the semantic breach rate as the primary indicator with the semantic refusal rate as its complementary reference, and characterizes the security boundary of the envelope layer through a two-by-two factorial experiment and a memory experiment. The results show that: when the envelope is authentic, strong labels consistently reduce the semantic breach rate, whereas weak labels are not necessarily better than an unlabeled baseline; when the envelope can be forged, the benefit of strong labels is weakened and its direction varies across frontends; and memory has a larger effect than any label manipulation: success memory raises the semantic breach rate while failure memory lowers it, and most combinations that contain both experiences return to the no-memory baseline. Accordingly, this paper refines the envelope layer from a single admission channel into two levels, an admission channel plus a context assembly surface, and argues that its security boundary is not a fixed property of labels but a dynamic outcome jointly shaped by label claims, frontend permissions, and historical memory.</p></details> |  |
| **[Constraining the Commit Boundary: A DIO/ODI-Informed Authorization Guardrail for Agentic Entity Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37407v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic Entity Resolution (ER) systems iteratively plan actions, retrieve evidence, revise hypotheses, and propose graph mutations. An erroneous identity hypothesis may condition later retrieval and produce apparent confirmation. This paper proposes a Digital Identity Optimization (DIO) and Ontology of Digital Identity (ODI)-informed authorization boundary between evidence available for agent reasoning and evidence eligible to authorize persistent identity-changing mutations. A domain-governed constraint-binding function maps selected reconstruction-failure distinctions to explicit identity-preserving constraints. An abstract two-phase supervisor permits a commit only when claim-specific authorization-eligible evidence supports every required proposition, relevant conflicts are resolved, the operation remains within its authorized scope, and relevant state and policy versions remain valid. Admission as UNVERIFIED does not confer authorization eligibility. Separate claim-specific promotion governs evidentiary use; insufficient support or unresolved material conflict leads to deferral. The contribution is a specification connecting diagnostic-to-constraint mapping, evidence promotion, proposition coverage, conflict handling, scope binding, and commit-time version integrity. A proposed evaluation protocol is designed to assess the mapping's incremental value beyond generic authorization and conventional identifier rules. | **[The Agent in the Middle: How AI-Mediated Access Reshapes Web Vulnerability Exploitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35541v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Web services increasingly expose backend interfaces that let users delegate tasks to AI agents, which invoke service operations directly without navigating websites. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. In this article, we apply to physics the collective predictive coding (CPC), the theory of language/symbol emergence. As our language emerges out of our human communications, physical macroscopic order parameter emerges out of communications among atoms --- each atom is regarded as an agent who predicts each other's behavior through inference. We present three examples of classical spin systems: two-dimensional ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic Ising models, a two-dimensional spin model with competing stripe orders, and the fully connected Sherrington--Kirkpatrick spin-glass model. In all of these examples, proper macroscopic order parameters including the replica overlap emerge automatically through communication informatics of CPC, only from observed spin configurations. We call the learned macroscopic coordinate the collective predictive order. Furthermore, for the ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic Ising models, the equilibrium distribution of the collective predictive order yields an emergent potential with the symmetry and thermal evolution characteristic of a Landau potential. Notably, the emergence of the collective predictive order does not refer to any structure of microscopic Hamiltonians/Lagrangians. Thus our ``Atom-as-an-Agent" (AaaA) principle offers a completely data-driven method for the emergence of macroscopic order, that is, machine learning physics.</p></details> | 33 pages, 10 figures |

| **[Matcha: Multi-Stage Riemannian Flow Matching for Accurate and Physically Valid Molecular Docking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14586v4)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Predicting a protein--ligand binding pose is central to structure-based drug design, yet current docking methods still struggle to combine speed, accuracy and physical plausibility. We introduce Matcha, which refines a ligand pose by three flow-matching stages in translation, rotation and torsion space, then minimizes, filters and ranks candidates with GNINA. Across the benchmarks considered, Matcha attains the strongest physical plausibility and runs about $28\times$ faster than modern large-scale co-folding models. Code and benchmarking artifacts are available at https://github.com/LigandPro/Matcha and archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23568093. | **[MLIPDiff: Guiding Denoising Diffusion Models for Structure-Based Drug Design via Machine-Learned Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33315v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion models have become a dominant approach to structure-based drug design. However, because they are trained primarily to fit reference structures, they often place ligands in physically implausible poses that clash with the protein. We introduce MLIPDiff, an inference-time guidance method that can be applied to pretrained diffusion models for ligand generation. The method uses physics-informed guidance from UMA (Universal Models for Atoms), a machine-learned interatomic potential (MLIP). The guidance combines four weighted energy terms (protein-ligand interaction, ligand strain, steric clash, and pocket anchoring) and uses the original model checkpoint without retraining. On CrossDocked2020, MLIPDiff completely eliminates protein-ligand collisions while preserving the ligand's local geometry, improving bond-length distributions and physical plausibility relative to the base model. Compared to models such as NucleusDiff that are specifically designed to reduce atomic clashes, MLIPDiff achieves clash-free structures without compromising local geometry. It maintains the base model's PoseBusters pass rate and attains the best median Vina Score, Vina Min, and Vina Dock, surpassing both the base model and NucleusDiff, with a median of 59.6\% of ligands per pocket binding more strongly than the reference. MLIPDiff thus addresses a limitation of prior methods by generating physically plausible structures with favorable predicted binding affinity using a single frozen model and no additional training.</p></details> |  |

Applying these diagnostics to current LLM-agent pipelines exposes a consistent failure mode: the agent performs molecular editing without knowing how the pocket-ligand complex responds to local modifications, thus rarely achieving joint improvement. To enable this evaluation, we introduce 3D-Fit - a token-efficient benchmarking strategy for assessing LLM performance on multi-conditioned spatial molecule generation. | **[SciCore-Mol: Augmenting Large Language Models with Pluggable Molecular Cognition Modules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22287v1)** | 2026-05-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are central to the one-for-all intelligent paradigm, but they face a fundamental challenge when dealing with heterogeneous scientific data such as molecules: the inherent gap between discrete linguistic symbols and topological molecular or continuous reaction data leads to significant information loss and semantic noise in text-based reasoning. Our experiments on diverse chemical tasks demonstrate that SciCore-Mol achieves strong comprehensive performance across molecular understanding, generation, reaction prediction, and general chemistry knowledge, with an 8B-parameter open-source system that is competitive with and in several dimensions surpasses proprietary large models. This work provides a systematic blueprint for equipping LLMs with scientific expertise through decoupled, pluggable, and flexibly orchestrated modules, with direct implications for drug design, chemical synthesis, and broader scientific discovery.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 4 figures, 9 tables. Preprint</p></details> |

| **[Generative Pseudo-Force Fields for Molecular Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19050v1)** | 2026-05-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generating stable molecular conformations typically forces a tradeoff between the physical realism of energy-based relaxation and the sampling efficiency of data-driven generative models. | **[Agentic reinforcement learning empowers next-generation chemical language models for molecular design and synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17687v2)** | 2026-01-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language models are revolutionizing the biochemistry domain, assisting scientists in drug design and chemical synthesis with high efficiency. Yet current approaches struggle between small language models prone to hallucination and limited knowledge retention, and large cloud-based language models plagued by privacy risks and high inference costs. To bridge this gap, we introduce ChemCRAFT, a novel framework leveraging agentic reinforcement learning to decouple chemical reasoning from knowledge storage. Instead of forcing the model to memorize vast chemical data, our approach empowers the language model to interact with a sandbox for precise information retrieval. This externalization of knowledge allows a locally deployable small model to achieve superior performance with minimal inference costs. To enable small language models for agent-calling ability, we build an agentic trajectory construction pipeline and a comprehensive chemical-agent sandbox. Based on sandbox interactions, we constructed ChemToolDataset, the first large-scale chemical tool trajectory dataset. Simultaneously, we propose SMILES-GRPO to build a dense chemical reward function, promoting the model's ability to call chemical agents. Evaluations across diverse aspects of drug design show that ChemCRAFT outperforms current cloud-based LLMs in molecular structure analysis, molecular optimization, and synthesis pathway prediction, demonstrating that scientific reasoning is not solely an emergent ability of model scale, but a learnable policy of tool orchestration. This work establishes a cost-effective and privacy-preserving paradigm for AI-aided chemistry, opening new avenues for accelerating molecular discovery with locally deployable agents. | **[Conditioned Generative Modeling of Molecular Glues: A Realistic AI Approach for Synthesizable Drug-like Molecules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18716v1)** | 2026-01-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Alzheimer's disease (AD) is marked by the pathological accumulation of amyloid beta-42 (Abeta-42), contributing to synaptic dysfunction and neurodegeneration. | **[Learning Logical Rules using Minimum Message Length](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06230v2)** | 2026-01-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Unifying probabilistic and logical learning is a key challenge in AI. We present in this work the first comprehensive experimental analysis of this advantage of QGANS applied to SAT4 image generation, obtaining an exponential advantage in capacity scaling for a quantum generator in the hybrid latent style-based QGAN architecture. Careful tuning of the autoencoder is crucial to obtain stable, reliable results. Once this tuning is performed and defining training optimality as when the training is stable and the FID score is low and stable as well, the optimal capacity (or number of trainable parameters) of the classical discriminator scales exponentially with respect to the capacity of the quantum generator, and the same is true for the capacity of the classical generator. ## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Provable Speedups From Dynamic Population Sizes in Evolutionary Algorithms for Multiobjective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23800v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper investigates the role of dynamic population sizes in evolutionary multi-objective optimization. Although such approaches are widely used in practice, their benefits remain poorly understood, and rigorous runtime analyses explaining when and why they help are still scarce. To address this, we introduce the bi-objective problem class $\text{CLIMB}$, where the parameter $\ell$ determines the size of the Pareto front. On this problem class, we analyze the runtime of GSEMO and the widely used NSGA-II. Our results show that allowing a dynamic population size for NSGA-II can lead to a moderate improvement, yielding a super-constant speedup for $\ell \in ω(\log(n)) \cap o(\sqrt{n \log(n)})$ in terms of fitness evaluations. In particular, we prove that GSEMO and NSGA-II-DYN, a version of NSGA-II with dynamic population sizes that we propose in this paper, can cover the Pareto front of $\text{CLIMB}$ in expected $O(n \log(n) + \ell^2 \log(en/(n-2\ell+3)))$ evaluations, and that this bound is tight for $\ell = O(\sqrt{n \log(n)})$. In contrast, NSGA-II with a fixed population size of at least $4\ell$ requires expected $O(n\ell + n\log(n) + \ell^2 \log(en/(n-2\ell+3)))$ evaluations to cover the entire Pareto front of $\text{CLIMB}$, while still requiring expected $Ω(\ell n)$ evaluations to even find a Pareto-optimal search point. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first rigorous runtime analysis in bi-objective optimization that demonstrates a super-constant speedup of GSEMO over NSGA-II. Our analysis builds on concepts from single-objective optimization, such as the evolution of population diversity over time, and employs the well-known family-tree method to prove the lower bound.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning Pareto Stationary Fronts via Single-Pass Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06397v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We propose MOSEL (Multi-Objective Stackelberg Efficient Learning), a framework for a posteriori multi-objective optimization (MOO) in deep neural networks that recovers a full front of Pareto stationary solutions at the computational cost of standard single-objective training. MOSEL reformulates the problem as a bilevel optimization problem that leverages network modularity to decouple representation learning from objective-preference alignment. Casting the bilevel optimization problem as a Stackelberg game enables solving the original a posteriori MOO problem in a single forward-backward pass. As a result, MOSEL matches the time and memory efficiency of standard single-objective training while enabling scalable Pareto stationary front learning. Empirically, MOSEL uncovers diverse and optimal Pareto frontiers in strongly conflicting settings (e.g., fairness-accuracy). Remarkably, even in weakly conflicting regimes such as multi-task learning, it consistently converges to solutions closer to the utopia point, outperforming both standard single-objective training and specialized multi-task learning methods. These results highlight the broader potential of a posteriori MOO learning as a pathway to efficiently learn more diverse and robust representations, ultimately improving generalization.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages. Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Camera-ready version</p></details> |
| **[Instant Software Factories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36254v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents can quickly build prototypes, but a long tail of fixes, optimizations, tests, and releases separates a prototype from a product. A software factory handles this ongoing work through a repeatable process. We ask whether this work needs dedicated orchestration and how such factories should be judged. Given a tested repository and a coding agent, an instant software factory uses only two coordination inputs, a swarm of cron-job agents with standing objectives and a shared communication board, such as GitHub, where agents coordinate through the traces they leave. We developed a bootstrap prompt designed to set up both inputs from one round of human input, with governance as a separable layer. We distilled it from two manually configured industrial factories, observed for 15 and 17 days, and have not yet evaluated it. In the GitHub-coordinated CLI factory, daily merges rose 15x during agent rollout, an observed association. Its agents also filed 95% of new issues and authored 40 of 58 PRs that changed CI workflows, and at least 5.6% of issues marked as fixed were later found to have incorrect fixes. Unlike an agent scored on a fixed benchmark, a factory thus changes its own workload, evaluator, and measured progress. We formulate factory development as multi-objective optimization under this endogenous feedback. Over five months, we plan to evolve both factories, test the bootstrap on new repositories, and test five falsifiable hypotheses.</p></details> |  |
| **[An Interactive Evolutionary Multiobjective Optimization Algorithm Based on Interpretable Active Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35470v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many decision-making tasks are inherently multi-objective. Interactive evolutionary multi-objective optimization algorithms (iEMOAs) are particularly well-suited to help the decision-maker find their most preferred solution. However, many of these algorithms employ black-box preference models. For high-stakes decision-making tasks, like healthcare and defense, a high level of transparency is needed, for instance to support accountability. We propose an interpretable iEMOA which uses active learning to efficiently learn a tree-based preference model directly from pairwise comparisons. The survival operator of NSGA-II and NSGA-III is extended with the learned preference model to guide the algorithm towards the region of interest. An extensive experimental study compares the proposed method to a state-of-the-art black-box model and a state-of-the-art interpretable model. Results indicate that our proposed model outperforms the alternatives in terms of finding the most preferred solution at the cost of a slightly worse average final population. Finally, a case study on a real-life engineering problem illustrates how inspecting the learned model reveals which objectives drive the decision-maker's preferences.</p></details> | 20 pages, 15 figures |
| **[KubePACS: Kubernetes Cluster Using Performant, Highly Available, and Cost Efficient Spot Instances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24027v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cloud users aim to minimize cost while maximizing performance by selecting the most suitable instance types for their workloads. To reduce expenses, spot instances have been widely adopted due to their steep discounts compared to on-demand pricing. However, their use introduces reliability risks due to potential interruptions, and existing research has primarily focused on mitigating this trade-off from a cost or availability perspective alone. Despite the diversity in hardware capabilities among instance types, current provisioning systems tend to ignore performance variation, selecting nodes solely based on minimum resource requirements. In this paper, we present KubePACS, a Kubernetes-native spot instance provisioning system that constructs node pools optimized for both cost and performance while guaranteeing high availability. KubePACS formulates the node selection process as a multi-objective optimization problem, incorporating real-time data such as spot prices, performance benchmarks, and availability scores, including the multi-node Spot Placement Score (SPS). It solves this problem efficiently using an Integer Linear Programming (ILP) approach guided by the Golden Section Search (GSS) algorithm to find the optimal configuration. By integrating with the Karpenter node autoscaler, KubePACS jointly optimizes instance-type selection and node scaling decisions within a standard provisioning workflow. KubePACS also adopts a novel heuristic to support workload-specific preferences by scaling performance metrics for specialized instances. Through extensive evaluation across synthetic and real-world workloads, KubePACS demonstrates on average 55.09% and up to 81.06% higher performance per dollar over state-of-the-art solutions such as Karpenter, SpotVerse, and SpotKube, which only reference the spot instance prices and limited availability data.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the 27th ACM International Middleware Conference (Middleware 2026)</p></details> |

To address this, we formulate the problem as a constrained optimization task and propose a novel three-stage analytical framework integrating semidefinite programming (SDP), augmented Lagrangian method (ALM), and second-order cone programming (SOCP), which enforces constraints more rigorously and achieves higher-quality solutions. First, we adopt a two-step SDP formulation to obtain a robust initialization in the non-convex search space. Next, we incorporate differentiable objectives and constraints into an incremental ALM to jointly handle coupled design constraints during global floorplanning. Finally, an SOCP-based legalization strictly enforces feasibility while further reducing area. Experiments on the FloorSet benchmark show that our method satisfies all constraints with the fastest runtime. Compared with state-of-the-art methods, it reduces wirelength and area by 90\% and 16\% on average, and achieves near-optimal results, with wirelength and area being merely 2\% and 1\% larger than the ground-truth references, respectively.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 7 figures, Accepted by International Conference on Computer-Aided Design (ICCAD) 2026</p></details> |
| **[ReMedI: Inference-Time Refinement and Self-Distillation for Controllable Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32103v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In drug development, lead optimization must improve competing properties, such as increasing permeability and reducing toxicity, while limiting scaffold changes that could compromise the lead's potency. Given a lead and a set of multi-objective tasks, the model proposes edits conditioned on the current molecule, while Metropolis--Hastings refinement guides sequential edits under an objective for property improvement, preservation, and lead similarity. | **[Cautious Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31855v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Partial differential equations (PDEs) are indispensable tools for modeling complex phenomena in physics and engineering. Despite their advantages, PINN training requires multi-objective optimization, which often leads to significant gradient conflicts among boundary, initial, and residual loss terms. The Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient (ConFIG) method addresses these conflicts; however, integrating ConFIG with adaptive optimizers such as Adam introduces a critical vulnerability: the conflict-free direction generated by ConFIG is further processed by the optimizer, and the resulting weight update may no longer be conflict-free with respect to the task gradients. To address this issue, Cautious ConFIG is proposed as a concise three-line extension to the ConFIG algorithm that empirically restores the conflict-freeness guarantee prior to the weight update. The method is evaluated on three PINN benchmarks: the 1D Viscous Burgers' equation, the 1D Nonlinear Schrödinger equation, and the 2D Kovasznay flow. Experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements over the standard ConFIG baseline. Additionally, the stabilizing effect of cautious masking and its reaction to conflict severity, task number, and optimizer lag are investigated using a simple toy system.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code available at [https://github.com/damiha/CautiousConFIG](https://github.com/damiha/CautiousConFIG)</p></details> |
| **[Can Broad Biomedical Knowledge be Contextualized into Scenario-Grounded Propositions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27082v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical discovery often requires connecting broad biomedical knowledge with specific experimental or clinical data. In clinical trials, SCENE discovers specific, well-supported subgroups and outperforms existing baselines. | **[Proprioceptive Safe Active Navigation and Exploration for Planetary Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08905v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deformable granular terrains introduce significant locomotion and immobilization risks in planetary exploration and are difficult to detect via remote sensing (e.g., vision). | **[Tradeoff-Aware Territory Design: A Multi-Objective Decision System for Enterprise Customer-to-Seller Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31701v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise customer journeys are shaped not only by product experiences and marketing, but also by recurring resource-allocation decisions. Directing scarce seller capacity toward higher-value accounts also offers incremental revenue potential at enterprise scale. | **[LOADBench: A Large Open Analog Dataset and Benchmark for Machine Learning in IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21591v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine learning (ML) for analog IC design is constrained by limited open data and the absence of standardized evaluation: results reported in different papers are typically obtained on bespoke, often closed, datasets and with ad-hoc metrics, leaving comparisons across methods unreliable. By exploiting the problem structure, we develop an efficient iterative algorithm with closed-form updates to solve the non-convex optimization problem with low complexity. Numerical results are provided to evaluate the performance of the proposed tri-HBF architecture in various setups. The results demonstrate that the proposed tri-HBF architecture can achieve significant improvement in energy efficiency (EE) compared to the conventional hybrid MIMO and DMA-only configurations, at the cost of minor degradations in sum rate and sensing mutual information.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, journal extention of our previous conference work</p></details> |
| **[Quantum Approximate Multi-Objective Optimization in Routing Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00168v1)** | 2026-09-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization (MOO) problems are common in logistics, where routing decisions must balance conflicting objectives such as travel distance, delivery time, and operational risk. A recently proposed Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) parameter-transfer strategy solves multi-objective MAX-CUT problems by reusing parameters trained on smaller instances, avoiding costly reoptimization for each scalarized problem. However, its effectiveness has only been demonstrated on proof-of-concept instances tailored to quantum hardware connectivity. In this work, we evaluate the applicability of this strategy to realistic routing problems. We formulate the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and Vehicle Routing Problem (VRP) as Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) models, reduce them to MAX-CUT, and assess the parameter-transfer framework under conditions matching the original study. Validation is performed through classical simulations and experiments on IBM quantum hardware. The resulting Pareto fronts are compared with those obtained using an adapted classical ε-constraint method, using hypervolume as the primary quality metric. Results indicate that parameter transfer remains effective, frequently achieving higher hypervolume and often finding competitive solutions earlier. However, performance depends on problem structure. The approach is consistently effective for TSP instances but less stable for the more constrained VRP, suggesting that QAOA parameter transferability decreases as the optimization landscape becomes more complex. | **[DQN-Scheduler: A Multi-Objective Optimization Framework for Scheduling Microservices in Cloud Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14117v1)** | 2026-09-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cloud computing has emerged as an information technology solution, providing software and infrastructure solutions for companies and individuals. The pay-as-you-go approach has increased demands for the cloud. The massive range of resources, the variety of services, and flexible pricing grab attention. In addition, microservices have emerged as a new way of building software, with applications developed as loosely dependent tasks. Additionally, container technology has boosted the popularity of microservices by offering a platform for this type of architecture. Containers and microservices improve the flexibility and scalability of cloud applications. There are two primary types of microservices: batch and online services, with the majority of applications falling into the online service category. Scheduling microservices is challenging because it requires careful management of resource utilization, load balancing, network latency, reliability, and availability. In this study, we introduce the DQN-Scheduler, a novel reinforcement learning-based agent designed to optimize microservice scheduling in cloud environments. Our approach aims to optimize multiple scheduling objectives simultaneously, such as resource utilization, load balancing, latency, reliability, and availability. To our knowledge, this is the first framework to address all these objectives simultaneously. The DQN-Scheduler was tested against benchmark algorithms in the field. The experimental results demonstrate that the DQN-Scheduler outperforms benchmark algorithms.</p></details> |  |

| **[GeoTTER: Leveraging Local Geometry of Optimal Transport for Zero-Shot Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13518v1)** | 2026-09-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present GeoTTER, a novel framework that redefines optimal transport in the realm of zero-shot classification. To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. | **[ATLAS: Automated Approximation of Transformers for Efficient Homomorphic Inference in One Hour](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23478v2)** | 2026-08-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fully homomorphic encryption (FHE) lets a server run inference on encrypted data with strong privacy guarantees, but running a Transformer under FHE is expensive. We present ATLAS, a training-free framework that automates this search by treating each layer's approximation setting as a multi-objective optimization over latency and accuracy. The problem is hard: the decision space is large (96 or 256 variables), each configuration takes 70 to 1,000 seconds to evaluate even in cleartext, and 85 to 90 percent of configurations are invalid. ATLAS handles this with a two-stage optimization strategy and a surrogate model, completing the search in about one hour. ## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Peak-DNA: Recurrent Morphology in Global Narrative Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36571v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Global narrative streams exhibit noisy, fragmented, and asynchronous amplification bursts, making direct waveform comparison unreliable for recurrence analysis. This paper introduces Peak-DNA, a framework for identifying recurrent propagation morphology in global narrative streams. The proposed framework transforms hourly GDELT activity into normalized macro-peak episodes through denoising, macro-peak consolidation, and temporal alignment, and compares propagation morphology using DTW-based similarity analysis. Using GDELT narrative streams, we analyze five high-amplification narrative domains: Iran, Defense, AI, Space/Elon, and Quantum/PQC. The results show that amplification events repeatedly converge toward morphology-level propagation patterns within each narrative domain. DTW-based similarity analysis further demonstrates strong intra-domain structural compactness despite heterogeneous publication dynamics and severe observational noise. These findings suggest a measurable morphology layer beyond semantic content or anomaly intensity, providing an initial structural foundation for recurrence-aware narrative monitoring, propagation family discovery, and longitudinal Narrative Genome analysis.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages. Accepted and presented at ASONAM 2026, Rabat, Morocco, August 24-27, 2026. Proceedings publication forthcoming</p></details> |
| **[CCP-DP: Cross-cluster context-guided phase dynamic programming for DNA storage sequence reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36559v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivation: DNA data storage offers high information density and long-term stability, but accurate sequence reconstruction remains challenging under limited sequencing coverage and strong insertion, deletion, and substitution (IDS) noise. Existing reconstruction methods mainly rely on local evidence within individual read clusters. When sequencing coverage is low or indel-induced phase shifts become pronounced, such local evidence may be insufficient to distinguish closely competing candidate sequences. Results: We present Cross-Cluster Context-Guided Phase Dynamic Programming (CCP-DP), which uses dataset-level cross-cluster information to complement cluster-local evidence and selectively refines ambiguous provisional consensuses. Under the main benchmark settings, CCP-DP achieved exact reconstruction rates of 95.36%-99.98% and outperformed existing methods on multiple datasets. At $5\times$ coverage, CCP-DP improved exact reconstruction by 6.2-17.5 percentage points over the stronger of BBS and CPL across the tested IDS conditions, while maintaining low reconstruction error and computational overhead. Overall, CCP-DP introduces cross-cluster shared information as a complementary source of evidence for DNA sequence reconstruction under challenging conditions. Availability: Source code is available at https://github.com/ZixinZ3899/CCP-DP.</p></details> |  |
| **[ACOR: incremental evidence accumulation towards real-time DNA storage reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36500v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>DNA offers high-density, durable storage by encoding digital information into synthetic strands and retrieving it through sequencing. Sequence reconstruction combines noisy copies of each strand before downstream decoding. Conventional offline methods typically reconstruct completed read clusters, separating reconstruction from ongoing read collection. Streaming reconstruction offers a way to overlap evidence processing with sequencing and reduce the computation remaining before downstream decoding. We present ACOR, a streaming consensus reconstruction method that records $k$-mer transitions and boundary support in a persistent ledger, then decodes through length-constrained beam search and priority-based selection at cluster closure. Evaluation on real and simulated DNA-storage datasets showed competitive recovery. On our own short-read next-generation sequencing (NGS) wet-lab library, ACOR combined near-complete recovery with second-scale reconstruction of more than ten million reads. A smaller public benchmark was completed in a fraction of a second. Parallel ACOR achieved speedups of tens to over a thousand-fold relative to the tested serial baseline configurations. Sparse storage reduced high-context memory requirements, while parallel execution accelerated reconstruction computation. ACOR is designed for streaming readout pipelines that process assigned reads while sequencing continues.</p></details> |  |

We therefore propose a mixture of non-negative Beta regression models estimated through an Expectation-Maximization algorithm for DNA methylation rates. Our framework naturally incorporates a feature selection mechanism through mixture component identification, making component selection a critical step of the inference procedure. We further propose a dedicated criterion for component selection and assess the performance of the approach through an extensive comparative study across several in vitro benchmark datasets. Our results demonstrate that deconvolution accuracy is highly sensitive to latent component structure and show that explicitly modeling this heterogeneity yields substantial improvements over standard whole-genome deconvolution strategies. Altogether, this work establishes mixture modeling of DNA methylation data as a powerful new direction for robust and accurate cell deconvolution.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rank Modulated Composite Encoding for Data Storage in DNA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00978v1)** | 2026-05-31 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper studies two problems that are motivated by combining two novel approaches, namely DNA composite and rank modulation. Under this paradigm, every composite symbols does not store a single nucleotide but a mixture of the four DNA nucleotides. Instead of considering all the possible composite symbols we are interested only in the rank of the motifs in the symbol. The first problem in this paper addresses the capacity of a channel that uses such symbols, while in the second, bounds and construction of such codes are studied.</p></details> |  |

In particular, for $t=1$ and $C>1/2$ we construct efficient codes with redundancy that essentially matches our non-constructive upper bound; b) $k$ equal a constant; in this case we consider the extremal problem where the number of deletions is not bounded and a deletion is imposed after every run of length at least $k$, which we call the extremal contextual deletion channel. This combinatorial setting arises naturally by considering a probabilistic channel that introduces contextual deletions after each run of length at least $k$ with probability $p$ and taking the limit $p\to 1$. Revised the statement and proof of the $t$-contextual deletion-correcting code in [v1, Theorem 4] (now Theorem 5). Constructed an alternative $t$-contextual deletion-correcting code with smaller redundancy but higher time complexity (Theorem 4). Added further details throughout the manuscript</p></details> |

| **[Can LLMs Uncover Hidden Physical Laws through Free Exploration?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37649v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific discovery often begins with an unexpected observation that prompts a new research question. In a world with altered physical laws, can large language models (LLMs) notice unexpected behavior, such as heavier objects falling more slowly, and discover the law through experiments without being told what to investigate? To evaluate this ability, we introduce LongChang, an automated framework that creates simulated worlds with altered physics, where one or more laws differ from ordinary physics. The evaluated LLMs act as explorers, manipulating objects and building tools to investigate their own questions without research tasks or hints about these laws. Separate LLM agents generate and review scenes, review tool designs, and evaluate reports for correctness and experimental support using the altered physical laws and exploration records. These records guide scene evolution for further exploration, allowing the full cycle to run without human intervention. Among 12 LLMs, GPT-6-Astra achieves a discovery rate of 16.7%, while the other 11 score 0%. The exploration records reveal uninvestigated anomalies and only partial identification of the altered physical laws.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://zhenyangcs.github.io/LongChang-website/</p></details> |
| **[Rare, Relevant, and Still Wrong: How Memory Fails LLM Group Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37624v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When an LLM group's discussion is compressed into a short memory for a final decision maker, what should the memory keep? We introduce CrossProfile, a diagnostic benchmark of 60 templates with automatically verified answer keys, in which the ownership of supporting, decoy, and irrelevant facts is crossed while their text is held fixed (480 tasks). Across Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and two DeepSeek models, both heuristics fail in predictable places. Selecting by relevance readmits decoys, because decoys are on topic: even a perfect relevance filter loses 23 to 31 points to rarest-first selection in the classic hidden-profile configuration. Rarest-first selection is no better than random selection on average and wins only where the rare facts happen to be the useful ones. In both cases accuracy follows the balance of evidence the memory keeps, not the policy that kept it. The decision model adds a third failure, a form of social proof: it gives extra weight to facts attributed to many agents. Removing the attribution tags, or reassigning them at random with line order fixed, raises accuracy in the classic configuration by 13 to 24 points. In natural discussions the evidence is lost before any memory acts: the complete transcript yields lower accuracy than no notes at all.</p></details> |  |

A candidate aggregation encoder summarizes offline candidate embeddings using K-means clustering and conditions the LLM on compact global candidate information; ranking then uses inner-product scores with cached candidate embeddings. A graph-based test-time scaling mechanism allocates additional inference computation to partition-conditioned query embeddings and ensembles their ranking scores. This separates the ability to score large candidate pools from the use of additional test-time computation to improve ranking quality. We evaluate LRanker on seven tasks across three scenarios in RBench, including a recommendation task with more than 6.8M candidates. Against the strongest baseline reported for each task, the relative MRR improvements in RBench-Large are 27.5%, 3.9%, 0.9%, and 8.9% on Rec-Movie, Rec-Toy, MS MARCO, and ESCI, respectively. On RBench-Small, the reported task scores improve by 26.7% on Rec-Music and 6.1% on Routing-Balance; on the 6.8M-candidate Rec-Clothing task, the improvement over Tiger is 27.0%. Ablations support the complementary contributions of candidate aggregation and test-time ensembling. | **[Correction or Manipulation? We examine this question across seven LLMs from four vendors in two studies, one on the answers agents give and one on the actions they take as customer-support agents whose policy permits account changes only for verified users. Simulated users push toward correct and incorrect answers with graded pressure. Following research on epistemic vigilance, the pressure either appeals to the source, such as claimed authority, or offers content the agent cannot verify, namely a cited source. Drawing on signal detection theory, we measure on a set of benchmark questions how much more readily each agent concedes to correction than to manipulation, separately from its overall propensity to concede. Citations made five of seven models readier to concede and four worse at discriminating, while stronger wording added little. In the agentic study, five models never violated the verification policy, including models that readily abandoned correct answers, so concession on answers did not predict violations of policy. Ministral 3B violated the policy mainly under claimed authority, and Ministral 8B reported changes it had not made. An instruction to revise only on verifiable evidence chiefly shifted propensity to concede rather than improving discrimination. Concession rates alone are therefore misleading, and agents should be evaluated on both their discrimination and the actions they take.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 7 figures, 11 tables. 8-page main paper with supplementary material included as an appendix. Under review</p></details> |
