| **[MUSE: Revealing Responsiveness Gaps of LLM Tutors on Multiple-Solution Programming Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36367v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When a problem admits multiple valid solutions, effective tutoring requires building on a student's strategy while introducing alternatives at appropriate points. | **[The Inbox as a Coordination Primitive for LLM Agent Societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36264v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language-model agents increasingly coordinate in large groups, and current agent protocols focus on delivering messages. For these agents, however, the scarce resource is not delivery but reading: every message costs tokens, and the context is finite. Current infrastructure works by push: the sender decides when a message enters the recipient's context, so the recipient reads in arrival order and drops the oldest messages once its context is full. We study the alternative, pull, in which messages wait in an inbox outside the context and the recipient's own rules decide what it reads and keeps. We argue that the inbox is a coordination primitive: each agent sets its rules privately, and those rules decide whether other agents' requests reach it. In simulated societies of up to 16,000 agents, with every decision an LLM judgment, we report three findings. First, push fails as an agent takes part in more coordination at once: an agent leading 20 negotiations with a full memory proposes options already rejected 97% of the time, because it has dropped the oldest replies. Second, an inbox is necessary under heavy, noisy traffic. Where first come, first read misses every request, an inbox that first reads messages carrying a refundable deposit still finds about 60% of the agents a request needs, at every size from 1,000 to 16,000. On a real email network, losses compound: when half the agents adopt such an inbox, forwarded search recovers only 5% of full adoption, against 42% for one-hop search. Third, an inbox comprises four decisions (what to read, what to keep, what a sender pays, and what a reply may say), and the best rule for each depends on the traffic and the agent's role. Multi-agent coordination under load requires an inbox layer designed for the traffic each agent receives.</p></details> |  |

Each specification is compiled into a symbolic finite automaton that incrementally consumes the resulting dialogue trace. Experiments on multi-turn safety benchmarks show that SpecGuard consistently outperforms representative open-source guard models while maintaining controlled false-positive rates. | **[Context Is a Pipeline, Not a Window: Information Attrition in LLM Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36114v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are judged by two numbers: the size of the context window, and how often the final answer is correct. | **[Adversary Brains: Hypothesis-Based Logical Signatures for Threat Actor Detection with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35624v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An adversary brain is an LLM-based model of a threat actor's behavior, held as an evolving logical signature: a hypothesis with enough structure that data can test it continuously, without a person re-interpreting it each time. Rather than any single observation, such as an IoC (Indicator of Compromise), it is the arrangement that becomes a detection, or intelligence, mechanism. Where YARA matches a file and Sigma matches a log line, an adversary brain matches the activity pattern of a campaign or threat actor. This is based on an intelligence analysis technique called Analysis of Competing Hypotheses (ACH), which the design lifts from the limitation of a few hypotheses at a time, to thousands, and then also operationalizes it for continuous detection purposes. Language models make the concept, and scale, practical: they turn prose reports into brains, judge matches, and drive the loop that keeps brains current. A partial pattern in a brain can also match a new value, such as a fresh C2 (command and control) domain name, assisting with the discovery of new indicators, and turning unknown unknowns into known unknowns, and potentially into knowns.
